			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Pixieset achieved 35% AI feature adoption by solving the right problem with Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-pixieset-achieved-35-ai-feature-adoption-by-solving-the-right-problem-with-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Kinman Lam]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:11:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c5a8d50fba3f5306158a47e53d9ac4f418c5cf16</guid>

					<description>Photographers are among the most skeptical audiences for generative AI. Learn how Pixieset used Amazon Bedrock to launch an AI-generated alt text feature to millions of users in four months, reaching 35% adoption by automating the tedious image SEO work photographers avoid, without touching the creative craft they take pride in.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Ry Rainey and Graham Gibson from Pixieset.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Photographers and artists are among the most skeptical audiences for generative AI. They have watched it threaten their craft and flood their industry with synthetic work. A 2025 MIT study found &lt;a href="https://mlq.ai/media/quarterly_decks/v0.1_State_of_AI_in_Business_2025_Report.pdf" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;95% of enterprise Generative AI pilots deliver zero measurable returns.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://pixieset.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Pixieset&lt;/a&gt; is an all-in-one photography business service providing photo galleries, websites, stores, studio management, and photo editing. Pixieset hosts over 8 billion photos and is trusted by millions of photographers worldwide. They cut through that skepticism by asking the right question: what tasks are our users forced to do that pull them away from their craft? The answer took them from concept to production in four months, drove significant subscription upgrades in week one, and has 35 percent of their applicable user base still using the feature sixteen months later.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each year, Pixieset runs a company-wide hackathon where teams experiment with new technology and explore what’s possible. It was through the hackathon that the team first saw what generative AI could do. When it came to product development, they brought that curiosity back to the customer problem.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://pixieset.com/website/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Pixieset Websites&lt;/a&gt; makes it straightforward for photographers to create a website for their business. The data showed that most photographer websites had little to no alt text. Alt text is a unique and accurate description for each image on a page. Without alt text, images are invisible to search engines. A typical photographer’s portfolio contains hundreds to thousands of images. Writing a unique and descriptive alt text for each image is tedious and time-consuming. Photographers know they should have alt text to get their business promoted, but the sheer volume of manual alt text entries makes it a task they perpetually defer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, Pixieset moved from concept to production launch of an AI image alt text generator to millions of users in 4 months. Graham Gibson, Staff Product Manager at Pixieset recalls, “In the first week alone, Pixieset generated alt text for over 750,000 photos, proving that this was a part of the workflow that photographers were happy to hand over to AI.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge"&gt;The challenge&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The challenge isn’t building the feature but driving its adoption. A tool has no value if users don’t trust it, or worse it alienates the creative professionals it’s designed to serve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;“We talked a lot about how this first AI feature must solve a real problem,” says Ry Rainey, Staff Engineer at Pixieset. Pixieset’s product philosophy has always been to identify moments where photographers spend time on work that isn’t photography and then alleviate that friction. The team applied this same lens to generative AI by not asking “what can this technology do?” but “where are our users losing time to tasks that are not creative?” That framing led the Pixieset team past flashier applications like AI-generated images, which would have encroached on the creative work photographers take pride in. They instead looked toward metadata, the invisible scaffolding that makes a portfolio visible to technologies and search engines but that no photographer enjoys writing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-18681-1.png" alt="Reviewing an AI-generated alt-text suggestion in the Pixieset website builder, with options to accept, edit, or reject" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: The AI-generated alt text review interface in the Pixieset website builder&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Rather than applying AI-generated alt-text across an entire website at once, the feature is available one image at a time. A photographer reviews a single suggested alt text, choosing to accept, edit, or reject it before expanding the scope. The photographer builds confidence in the output quality at their own pace. When the photographer is comfortable, they enable “auto-apply” across their portfolio, and even then, every caption remains editable. The photographer retains final say, and the AI earns trust incrementally.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the end-to-end flow for Pixieset’s AI-generated alt text feature. Pixieset is built on event-driven architecture using Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2), AWS Lambda, and Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-18681-2.png" alt="End-to-end flow of the AI-generated alt-text feature, from photo upload through Amazon Bedrock inference to caption review" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: End-to-end flow for Pixieset’s AI-generated alt text feature&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a photographer uploads an image, an event is triggered. For this AI-generated alt text feature, the Pixieset team added one more step to that existing worker pipeline by sending the image to Amazon Bedrock for inference by a multimodal large language model (LLM), storing the generated caption alongside the photo, and surfacing it for review within the website builder photographers already use daily.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This integration required only a single API call without new infrastructure, GPU provisioning, or model hosting. “This is one of the primary reasons we chose Amazon Bedrock,” says Graham. By using the fully managed Amazon Bedrock with secure access to leading foundation models through a single API, Pixieset scaled from zero to 750,000 inference requests in the first week without provisioning a single server.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Pixieset team needed the feature to be highly available because if a user sees empty generative AI captions or “please try again” errors, it erodes trust and hinders the adoption of the feature. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/getting-started-with-cross-region-inference-in-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Cross-Region inference in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; automatically routes requests across Regions within a defined geographic boundary, maintaining throughput without requiring Pixieset to build routing logic. The Pixieset team took it one step further by catching failures from the first model and retrying with a secondary model of similar quality. As a result, this feature has had zero downtime since launch.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pixieset launched the feature at the beginning of 2025 using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/claude/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Anthropic Claude 3.5 Sonnet&lt;/a&gt; through Amazon Bedrock, selecting it for its multimodal image understanding, fast inference, and cost efficiency at scale. Because Amazon Bedrock presents a unified API across model providers, Pixieset evaluated newer models without locking into a particular version or provider and swap them without re-architecting the integration. The advanced prompt optimization in Amazon Bedrock further lowers the switching cost by adapting existing prompts to a new model’s strengths automatically. Since launch, the Amazon Bedrock model catalog has continued to expand, including the latest &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-claude-sonnet-5-on-aws-anthropics-most-capable-sonnet-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Sonnet 5&lt;/a&gt; as of June 2026. It also includes &lt;a href="https://openai.com/index/openai-on-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;OpenAI’s GPT-5.5&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt;, and models from Meta, Mistral, Cohere and many more. This means the feature they shipped in 2025 continues to improve as the underlying models do, with minimal engineering investment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Response&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Upon launching the feature, the response from photographers confirmed they had asked the right question and built something truly useful:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“OMG Pixieset! Okay, I don’t normally like the use of AI, but using AI to mass-generate image descriptions is absolutely fantastic. Obviously I’m checking through to approve them, but this is a game-changer!”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“This new ’SEO for images’ generative AI alt-text for all pictures on website is great!! Saves me so much time. Very few minor tweaks needed here and there but overall accurate. Excellent feature Pixieset!”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“I updated my subscription to benefit from this innovation &lt;img src="https://s.w.org/images/core/emoji/14.0.0/72x72/1f642.png" alt="&#128578;" class="wp-smiley" style="height: 1em; max-height: 1em;"&gt; thank you for your work”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="three-principles-builders-can-apply"&gt;Three principles builders can apply&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Pixieset’s success with AI-generated alt text wasn’t a matter of luck or timing. Three design decisions, made before a line of code was written, shaped the outcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="categorize-your-ai-features-into-moats-and-must-haves"&gt;Categorize your AI features into moats and must-haves&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Not every AI feature deserves the same level of investment. Pixieset recognized early that AI-generated alt text is a must-have, not a proprietary moat that deepens over time with unique data. It’s a table-stakes capability that most website builders will eventually offer. That distinction told the team to prioritize speed to market instead of over-engineering. That focus is how they moved from concept to production in four months rather than spending a year building differentiation that wouldn’t compound.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="solve-work-that-isnt-their-craft"&gt;Solve work that isn’t their craft&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The most adoption-resistant AI features are the ones that encroach on work users take pride in. Pixieset deliberately chose to automate metadata, the invisible scaffolding that makes a portfolio discoverable, rather than anything that touches the creative act of photography itself. The result was that photographers didn’t feel replaced but relieved of a chore they’d been neglecting, which is why the feature drives subscription upgrades rather than cancellations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="let-users-build-trust-at-their-own-pace"&gt;Let users build trust at their own pace&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Even a well-targeted feature can fail if it asks users to hand over too much control at once. Pixieset introduced the AI one image at a time, letting photographers review a single caption before deciding whether to expand scope to their full portfolio. That patience paid off, and 35 percent of users eventually chose full automation on their own terms. They arrived at that confidence through direct product experience rather than a product team’s assurance.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The most striking thing about Pixieset’s AI-generated alt text feature is how little infrastructure it required. A single Amazon Bedrock inference call, embedded into an existing event-driven pipeline, delivered a feature with 35 percent user adoption that shows no signs of plateauing. But the technical simplicity is the lesser lesson. What made it succeed was the discipline of knowing which problem to solve, whose work to automate, and how much control to hand over at once.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Get started with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/getting-started/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; to build your first inference-powered feature. When you’re ready to move into multi-step agentic workflows, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; provides the managed infrastructure to scale from there. If you’re building with AI from the start, the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/devops/open-sourcing-adaptive-workflows-for-ai-driven-development-life-cycle-ai-dlc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AI-Driven Development Lifecycle (AI-DLC)&lt;/a&gt; offers a framework for using AI as a collaborator across every phase of software development.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>First Orion accelerates QA automation using Amazon Nova Act</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/first-orion-accelerates-qa-automation-using-amazon-nova-act/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Avinash Ranganath]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 11 Aug 2026 16:09:06 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Nova]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3896c2214d548edf11e4411ce17c3d1bc983082a</guid>

					<description>Learn how First Orion, a branded communications company, shifted from brittle script-based UI testing to AI-driven QA automation with Amazon Nova Act. By describing tests in plain English instead of maintaining selector-based code, they cut QA cycle times, freed engineering capacity, and caught regressions earlier.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Mark Himelfarb and Garrett Wilkerson from First Orion.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://firstorion.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;First Orion&lt;/a&gt;’s engineering teams were shipping faster than quality assurance (QA) could test, until Amazon Nova Act transformed QA automation. As a branded communications company whose solutions reach hundreds of millions of phone calls across carriers in the US, Canada, UK, and Germany, First Orion needed their QA to keep pace with rapid development velocity. The solution was a shift from script-based test automation to agents driven by AI that understand web interfaces the way a human does. This post describes how First Orion adopted Amazon Nova Act, the architecture they built around it, and the results they achieved.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://firstorion.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;First Orion&lt;/a&gt; was founded in 2008 with a single belief: every communication should be clear, trusted, and recognized. Today they have over 300 team members across offices in North Little Rock, Seattle, London, and Dubai. Their solutions run across all major US carriers including T-Mobile, Verizon, AT&amp;amp;T, and &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/press-releases/first-orion-boost-mobile-inform-branded-calling" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Boost Mobile&lt;/a&gt;, major Canadian carriers, &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/press-releases/vodafone-uk-branded-calling-launch" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Vodafone&lt;/a&gt; in the UK, and &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/press-releases/first-orion-deutsche-telekom-branded-calling-germany" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Deutsche Telekom&lt;/a&gt; in Germany. Coverage is increasing with their &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/press-releases/first-orion-previews-april-2026-global-exchange" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Global Exchange&lt;/a&gt;. They help businesses connect with customers through branded calling, messaging, and identity solutions while protecting both parties from spam, scam, and spoofing. They serve a wide range of customers, from small businesses to global enterprises, including Fortune 500 companies. Their product suite covers the full communications lifecycle: &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/inform" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;INFORM Branded Calling&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/enrich" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ENRICH Branded Messaging&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/affirm" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AFFIRM Number Monitoring&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/sentry" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;SENTRY Call Blocking&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://firstorion.com/products/protect-risk-detection" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;PROTECT+ Risk Detection.&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;They believe the future of communication is built on trust, transparency, and intelligent technology, and innovation is central to their strategy. They invest in AI, conversational intelligence, and data-driven platforms to continuously improve how brands engage with their customers. That growth created an engineering challenge. As First Orion expanded into the small and medium-sized business (SMB) market alongside their enterprise customers, their web applications multiplied, and so did the QA burden of testing them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decentralized-portals-outpaced-qa-team"&gt;Decentralized portals outpaced QA team&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion moved from a monolithic web portal to a decentralized, product-specific, modular, cell-based architecture. In this model, each team owns the applications for its own line of business and synchronizes only over a small, shared piece of the underlying platform. This let teams move in parallel at higher velocity, though it required substantial re-engineering of the platform to support. At the same time, as First Orion pushed further into the SMB market, the range of device form-factors and browser versions their customers used grew significantly, multiplying the combinations every application had to be tested against.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;UI testing quickly became the bottleneck. QA teams could not keep up with the sprawling set of web applications, and the consequences compounded: release velocity stalled, new features shipped with lower quality, and engineering time was increasingly consumed by regressions rather than new capabilities.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion’s QA team includes both QA Analysts and QA Automation Engineers. Automation Engineers build durable test suites from test case definitions and other documentation, while QA Analysts focus on requirement analysis, test case definition, exploratory testing, and software-quality risk management. But the incumbent test automation frameworks they relied on were inherently fragile, and three problems in particular slowed the team down.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, regression testing was not self-service. Developers needed a straightforward way to run regressions across the paths they touched before shipping to the test environment, but the existing test cases carried extensive dependencies that made them difficult to run on demand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Second, new features suffered from a test case gap. To write tests for a new feature, that feature first had to be developed and deployed to the test environment, so that QA Automation Engineers had the selectors, labels, and other elements they needed in the Document Object Model (DOM). This forced QA Analysts and Engineers to context-switch to other work while waiting, and with so many features shipping in rapid succession, the result was mounting cognitive load and constant dependency wrangling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Third, the test scripts themselves were brittle. QA Automation Engineers typically received applications only after developers finished the code, always close to release. As they worked through Selenium and Playwright scripts, element IDs, classes, and other DOM and JavaScript attributes changed faster than they could fix them. Traditional automation was not augmenting the manual effort quickly enough to matter for a given release, and releases could slip as a result. Increasing the size of the QA team helped at the margins but did not address this root cause.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion realized they did not need more of the same. They needed a fundamentally different approach. Instead of writing code that describes how to navigate a UI, they wanted to describe what to test in plain English and have an intelligent agent handle the rest. That requirement led them to Amazon Nova Act.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-nova-act-fits-first-orions-requirements"&gt;Why Amazon Nova Act fits First Orion’s requirements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion’s requirements came down to two problems: selector-based tests that broke as the UI changed, and the time it took to author new ones. Nova Act addressed both by letting QA Analysts describe tests in plain English rather than write and maintain code. When their AWS account team introduced them to Nova Act in March 2025, First Orion became a pre-release adopter and found value well before general availability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With Nova Act, First Orion describes what they want accomplished in natural language, not how to accomplish it. For example: “Log into the portal, navigate to billing, and verify the invoice total.” The agent reasons about the current UI state, identifies elements, and runs multi-step sequences autonomously. This was the critical difference from Selenium and Playwright. Those frameworks required QA Automation Engineers to write and maintain explicit element selectors that broke with every sprint. Nova Act instead reasons about what it sees on screen: labels, layout, and context. A Nova Act instruction like “click the Submit button” works regardless of the underlying CSS class. The model adapts to changing page layouts, handles dynamic content, dismisses pop-ups, and recovers from errors without human intervention. Developers can also interleave Python code, assertions, breakpoints, and parallelization directly with Nova Act commands.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For First Orion, this meant three things:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;QA Analysts could author tests directly&lt;/strong&gt; — no waiting for automation engineers to translate test cases into code.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tests survived UI changes&lt;/strong&gt; — because Nova Act does not rely on fixed selectors, scripts no longer broke every sprint.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No browser infrastructure to manage&lt;/strong&gt; — AgentCore Browser handles provisioning, session recording, and parallel execution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-first-orion-uses-nova-act"&gt;How First Orion uses Nova Act&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion’s main use case for Amazon Nova Act is testing their customer portal applications. They built an end-to-end system around the Amazon Nova Act SDK that takes QA analysts from English-language test case authoring through automated execution to integrated reporting. The following diagram shows the architecture, and we walk through each component below.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-20843-1.png" alt="First Orion Nova Act QA automation architecture: Test Case Authoring UI, Amazon S3 test store, Nova Act Test Runner on Amazon ECS with AWS Fargate, AgentCore Browser, and Allure reporting dashboard" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Architecture overview of First Orion’s Nova Act test automation system&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The workflow begins with the Test Case Authoring UI, a React frontend where QA analysts browse, create, edit, and validate test cases in plain English. A custom templating engine handles dynamic variables (phone numbers, email addresses, business names) so the same test collection generates unique, realistic data on every run. Test collections are stored as JSON in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon S3), which serves as the central repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a QA analyst triggers a test run, the Nova Act Test Runner picks up test cases from Amazon S3. This runner is a Python application on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon ECS) with AWS Fargate that orchestrates execution through the Amazon Nova Act SDK. The runner does not manage browsers directly; instead, it delegates browser operations to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/browser-tool.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Browser&lt;/a&gt;. AgentCore Browser provisions managed browser instances, handles session recording, and supports parallel execution behind multi-factor authentication (MFA) without requiring changes to the target application’s auth flow. Browser profiles can start tests in a particular browser state, avoiding full login flows for every run.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon Nova Act model receives natural language instructions from the test runner, reasons about the target web application’s current UI state, and executes the specified actions autonomously. Nova Act handles the browser manipulation on its own, the calling code does not need to manage selectors, waits, or page transitions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Results flow into an &lt;a href="https://allurereport.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;Allure&lt;/a&gt; reporting dashboard with a Microsoft Teams integration for notifications. First Orion relies on AgentCore for session recording and links video replays directly in their reports. The Microsoft Teams and Allure required custom integration. The Nova Act test runner used a Python script to orchestrate the SDK calls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Adopting this architecture has tightened integration between the QA and engineering teams. As more testing moves into the agentic flow, dependence on other vendors shrinks. First Orion also adopted the Kiro IDE for prototyping and development. Kiro’s built-in knowledge of AWS services and ability to read code bases made wiring everything up straightforward.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows an example test case as authored in the React UI. QA analysts write instructions in plain English (for example, “Navigate to the Billing page, verify the invoice total matches the expected amount, and download the PDF”). The Amazon Nova Act SDK translates these into browser actions at runtime without requiring element selectors or programming knowledge from the test author.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-20843-2.png" alt="Test Case Authoring UI showing a plain-English test case in the React frontend" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Test case authored in plain English in the React UI&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Allure dashboard provides test-level pass/fail status, execution timelines, and links to AgentCore Browser session recordings for debugging failed tests.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-20843-3.png" alt="Allure dashboard showing test pass and fail status, execution timelines, and links to session recordings" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Allure reporting dashboard&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/04/ML-20843-4.png" alt="Allure test results view listing individual test case outcomes" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Allure test results&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before Nova Act, a QA Automation Engineer would manually identify DOM selectors, write scripts, debug brittle selectors, and iterate. This process can take days per test case. With this architecture, a QA Analyst takes the same English-language test case and runs it within minutes. The turnaround from test case definition to automated run dropped from days to minutes for supported scenarios.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-impact"&gt;Results and impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;While Amazon Nova Act is still in its adoption period and First Orion is adding features around it to fit their environment, they have seen as much as 20–25 percent reduction in QA cycles for specific types of testing. As coverage expands to additional application modules, they expect further efficiency gains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;They estimate saving 25–30 percent of Engineering time with Nova Act, decreasing the amount of context switches between features. Engineering capacity that was previously needed to support the QA function has been redirected to feature engineering and value creation, allowing First Orion to focus on serving their customers better.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Fully automated Nova Act runs allow for early regression detection and quick new feature testing, increasing their test coverage by upwards of 15 percent in some cases. By running tests against every build without waiting for manual QA availability, First Orion catches regressions earlier in the development cycle, before they reach production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned-and-best-practices"&gt;Lessons learned and best practices&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizational buy-in matters. First Orion’s culture of trying new things and shipping quickly made adoption possible. Teams considering agentic automation need leadership support and room to experiment. For teams considering agentic automation: have a plan but stay flexible. This space moves fast and what failed last month can work today with a new model update.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One early challenge was the predictability of the model’s navigation path. First Orion’s QA engineers wanted to confirm the agent took the same route through the site given the same preconditions, but initially Nova Act would reach the target state by different paths. By tuning parameters exposed through the Amazon Nova Act SDK, they significantly improved behavioral consistency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Another learning was the importance of clear prompts. While English test cases can be written for the SDK, the prompts still needed tuning to work well with the model. Following the AWS documentation guidance on structuring prompts increased test reliability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A related but distinct issue was that AI agents do not share institutional knowledge. Unspoken preconditions, navigation patterns, and business rules must be explicitly provided in prompts. Building this context into test case definitions was key to achieving consistent results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion’s roadmap with Amazon Nova Act is ambitious. Their next step for the QA-specific use case includes automated test generation using Kiro and Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers that give AI tools access to code changes and requirements documents. By having Kiro see code changes and check requirements, First Orion envisions a fully automated test generation pipeline that feeds directly into their Nova Act implementation, further freeing QA Engineers for higher-value tasks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;They also plan to use Nova Act for critical path analysis, understanding how AI navigates their sites to identify where products can improve. Feeding this information into other systems can provide feedback to web designers and marketing teams, helping them build more efficient webpages that lead to better customer experiences.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion’s adoption of Amazon Nova Act demonstrates how agentic AI can transform QA workflows from a bottleneck into a competitive advantage. By shifting from brittle, selector-based automation to natural language-driven testing, First Orion reduced QA cycle times, freed engineering capacity for feature development, and improved their ability to catch regressions early. Their willingness to adopt new technology early and their close collaboration with our team as a launch partner positioned them to move faster and build a testing architecture that scales with their growing product portfolio.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First Orion CTO Mark Himelfarb sums it up:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“This isn’t just a tool. It’s a competitive edge. Tasks that took hours now take minutes, so we keep standards high while we scale.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Mark Himelfarb, CTO, First Orion&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ready to get started? Visit &lt;a href="https://nova.amazon.com/act?tab=playground" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nova Act Playground&lt;/a&gt; to prototype your first workflow without writing code. When you’re ready to build, install the &lt;a href="https://labs.amazon.science/blog/nova-act-extension-build-and-test-ai-agents-without-leaving-your-ide" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Nova Act IDE extension&lt;/a&gt; for VS Code, Kiro, or Cursor to develop and deploy agents from your IDE. For a production-ready architecture guide, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/agentic-qa-automation-using-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-browser-and-amazon-nova-act/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agentic QA Automation using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Browser and Amazon Nova Act&lt;/a&gt;, and refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/nova-act/latest/userguide/what-is-nova-act.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova Act documentation&lt;/a&gt; for more details.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Deploying Anthropic Claude apps gateway for AWS for enterprise workloads</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploying-anthropic-claude-apps-gateway-for-aws-for-enterprise-workloads/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dani Mitchell]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 11 Aug 2026 15:59:22 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">553759b28ce6c7119699e64693bcdea7573c4ffd</guid>

					<description>Claude apps gateway is a self-hosted governance layer between Claude Code and Claude Desktop and Amazon Bedrock or Claude Platform on AWS. This post presents a production reference deployment covering end-to-end architecture, enterprise deployment patterns, cost, and implementation resources.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;AI administrators deploying &lt;a href="https://claude.com/product/claude-code"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://claude.com/product/overview"&gt;Claude Desktop&lt;/a&gt; across their workforce need centralized controls over authentication, model access, cost attribution, and spend enforcement. These controls reduce operational overhead and apply governance consistently at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway"&gt;Claude apps gateway&lt;/a&gt; provides a self-hosted governance layer between these applications and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/claude-platform/"&gt;Claude Platform on AWS&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Building on our &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-claude-apps-gateway-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;launch post&lt;/a&gt;, this post presents a production reference deployment covering end-to-end architecture, enterprise deployment patterns, cost, and implementation resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section covers the reference deployment topology and how requests flow through the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deployment-topology"&gt;Deployment topology&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Claude apps gateway ships in the same &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/quickstart"&gt;Claude Code CLI&lt;/a&gt; binary that developers already run. Started with &lt;code&gt;claude gateway --config gateway.yaml&lt;/code&gt;, it runs in server mode and loads its YAML configuration at startup. In this reference deployment, the container runs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fargate/"&gt;AWS Fargate&lt;/a&gt; inside your &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/"&gt;virtual private cloud&lt;/a&gt; (VPC). The same image can run on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon EKS) or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ec2/"&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon EC2) if either better matches your existing setup.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reference architecture uses the following components:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compute and state:&lt;/strong&gt; Each AWS Fargate task runs one stateless gateway container. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/rds/"&gt;Amazon Relational Database Service&lt;/a&gt; (Amazon RDS) for PostgreSQL stores short-lived sign-in state, including device codes and sessions. When spend limits are enabled, it also stores per-user spend counters and audit records. Auth state lives in the database rather than in a task. This means any task can serve any request, with no sticky sessions required on the load balancer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Ingress and private DNS:&lt;/strong&gt; An internal &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/elasticloadbalancing/application-load-balancer/"&gt;Application Load Balancer&lt;/a&gt; terminates TLS using an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/certificate-manager/"&gt;AWS Certificate Manager&lt;/a&gt; certificate. An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/"&gt;Amazon Route 53&lt;/a&gt; private hosted zone resolves the gateway to private IP addresses reachable through a VPN, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/directconnect/"&gt;AWS Direct Connect&lt;/a&gt;, or equivalent private connectivity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service connectivity:&lt;/strong&gt; VPC endpoints keep supported AWS service traffic private, while a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/vpc/latest/userguide/vpc-nat-gateway.html"&gt;NAT gateway&lt;/a&gt; provides other required egress.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Upstream credentials:&lt;/strong&gt; The gateway authenticates to Amazon Bedrock using the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management&lt;/a&gt; (IAM) role assigned to the gateway task. The Claude Platform on AWS API key and other static credentials remain in &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/secrets-manager/"&gt;AWS Secrets Manager&lt;/a&gt;. No upstream credentials are distributed to developer machines.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational note:&lt;/strong&gt; Configure the load balancer idle timeout to exceed the longest expected interval without data. The default is 60 seconds. The load balancer terminates connections that remain idle longer than the configured timeout. Check both delayed non-streaming responses and pauses between streaming chunks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram showing developers reaching AWS Fargate gateway tasks through an internal Application Load Balancer, with Amazon RDS for state and upstreams to Amazon Bedrock and Claude Platform on AWS" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Reference architecture for Claude apps gateway on AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="request-flow"&gt;Request flow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sign-in (once per session).&lt;/strong&gt; The platform team distributes &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway#connect-developers" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;managed settings&lt;/a&gt; that point Claude Code and Claude Desktop to the gateway’s private URL. When a developer runs &lt;code&gt;/login&lt;/code&gt;, the client starts the OAuth 2.0 device authorization grant and opens a browser to authenticate through your &lt;a href="https://openid.net/developers/how-connect-works/"&gt;OpenID Connect&lt;/a&gt; (OIDC) identity provider. The browser must also reach the gateway’s private endpoint because the gateway serves the device verification page. After authentication, the gateway issues a short-lived bearer token, valid for one hour by default. The session subsequently refreshes silently in the background.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference (every request).&lt;/strong&gt; Every inference request carries the bearer token. The gateway validates it, resolves the developer’s identity and group membership, applies the matching policy, evaluates the applicable spend cap, and routes the request to Amazon Bedrock or Claude Platform on AWS. The response streams back to the client. The client emits usage metrics, which the gateway forwards over the OpenTelemetry Protocol (OTLP) to a collector you configure. The metrics are attributed to the authenticated identity used for policy evaluation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For deployment scripts and configuration templates, refer to the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/anthropic-on-aws/tree/main/claude-apps-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying repository&lt;/a&gt;. For operational guidance, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-deploy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;deployment guide&lt;/a&gt;. For device code verification and token lifecycle details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude apps gateway documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="what-it-solves"&gt;What it solves&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway addresses five governance needs, each described in the following sections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="identity-sso-authentication"&gt;1. Identity: SSO authentication&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway delegates authentication to your OIDC identity provider. Developers sign in once through browser SSO. The gateway issues a short-lived token and handles silent refresh in the background. The gateway supports OIDC-approved providers including &lt;a href="https://www.okta.com/"&gt;Okta&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/business/identity-access/microsoft-entra-id"&gt;Microsoft Entra ID&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://auth0.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Auth0&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.keycloak.org/"&gt;Keycloak&lt;/a&gt;, or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/cognito/?trk=4cc82392-4dab-406f-91c8-96680bbf531f&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=CjwKCAjwpqHTBhAcEiwAj2Afui4ZVRj0ExkCug9kd9UjQQiWUUzkm7uaDb_Fe4c7-G0YsZHzj2eKgBoCAvIQAvD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23528572748&amp;amp;gads_ag=198422384291&amp;amp;gads_ad=795811958064&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20cognito&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9042989&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23528572748&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp8i14OymISrCSMhtAjqFD58j&amp;amp;gclid=CjwKCAjwpqHTBhAcEiwAj2Afui4ZVRj0ExkCug9kd9UjQQiWUUzkm7uaDb_Fe4c7-G0YsZHzj2eKgBoCAvIQAvD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Cognito&lt;/a&gt;, for example.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This gives you centralized OIDC authentication with no upstream credentials on developer machines, instant offboarding through identity provider removal, and consistent per-user attribution across requests without custom instrumentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway keeps no user directory of its own. There are no accounts to pre-create and no SCIM sync to configure. Whatever groups your identity provider assigns to a user are the groups the gateway uses for policy matching, 1:1 with no translation layer. Manage users and groups entirely in your identity provider, and the gateway picks up changes at the next session refresh. Offboarding is removing the user from your identity provider. Their session expires within the configured time to live (1 hour by default), with no credential rotation required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following example shows a gateway configured with Microsoft Entra ID:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;oidc:
  issuer: https://login.microsoftonline.com/&amp;lt;tenant-id&amp;gt;/v2.0
  client_id: ${OIDC_CLIENT_ID}
  client_secret: ${OIDC_CLIENT_SECRET}
  allowed_email_domains: [company.com]
  groups_claim: roles&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Microsoft Entra ID doesn’t include group or role claims by default. If your policies use &lt;code&gt;match: {groups: [...]}&lt;/code&gt; with Entra app roles, add &lt;code&gt;groups_claim: roles&lt;/code&gt; to your OIDC configuration. Without this step, the gateway cannot resolve group membership and all users match only the catch-all policy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For per-identity-provider setup instructions, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-deploy#identity-provider-setup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;identity provider setup guide&lt;/a&gt;. The following images show the sign-in experience from the developer’s perspective, for both Claude Code and Claude Desktop.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-2.png" alt="Claude Code terminal prompting the developer to authenticate through the gateway" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Authenticating through the gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-3.png" alt="Browser showing the OIDC identity provider sign-in page presented by the gateway" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: The gateway delegates to your existing OIDC identity provider&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-4.png" alt="Browser device-authorization page confirming the device code for the gateway" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Authorizing the device in the browser&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 1128px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/Screenshot-2026-08-06-at-11.58.33 AM.png" alt="Claude Desktop settings configured to route inference through the gateway to Amazon Bedrock" width="1118"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Configuring inference with Amazon Bedrock through the gateway for Claude Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="policy-centralized-model-access-and-permissions"&gt;2. Policy: Centralized model access and permissions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway enforces model access server-side and distributes tool permissions as managed settings, scoped by identity provider group. You define which models and capabilities each team gets in a single YAML block. Policies are evaluated in declaration order. The first match is selected, then merged onto the &lt;code&gt;match: {}&lt;/code&gt; catch-all base. End the list with a &lt;code&gt;match: {}&lt;/code&gt; policy. It acts as a catch-all for users whose groups don’t match a specific policy earlier in the list. Without one, unmatched users receive full catalog access. Changes propagate to connected clients within one hour, requiring no action from developers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;Managed:
  policies:
    # Contractors: Haiku only, no web access
    - match: { groups: [contractors] }
      cli:
        availableModels: [claude-sonnet-5, claude-haiku-4-5]
        enforceAvailableModels: true
      permissions:
        deny: ["WebFetch", "WebSearch"]
    # Engineers: full model access with guardrails
    - match: { groups: [engineers] }
      cli:
        availableModels: [claude-opus-4-8, claude-sonnet-5, claude-haiku-4-5]
      permissions:
        allow: [Read, Grep, Bash, Edit]
        deny: ["Read(./.env)", "Read(./secrets/**)"]
    # Catch-all: every other authenticated user. Must be last.
    - match: {}
      cli:
        availableModels: [claude-haiku-4-5, claude-sonnet-5]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Include &lt;code&gt;desktop: {}&lt;/code&gt; in every policy entry to turn on Claude Desktop clients. Without it, the gateway rejects Desktop inference requests for users matching that policy, even though sign-in succeeds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model access is enforced server-side. A developer whose group only grants Claude Haiku cannot bypass the restriction, even with a modified client. The model picker in Claude Code and Claude Desktop shows only permitted models. For the full policy schema including tool permissions and managed settings delivery, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-config#managed" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;configuration reference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figures show policy enforcement in action.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-6.png" alt="Claude Code returning a 400 error when a contractor-group user requests Claude Opus 4.8" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: A user in the contractors group receives a 400 error when requesting Claude Opus 4.8 in Claude Code&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-7.png" alt="Claude Desktop model picker showing only Haiku available to the same contractor-group user" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: The same user can access only Claude Haiku in Claude Desktop&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="telemetry-per-user-usage-attribution"&gt;3. Telemetry: Per-user usage attribution&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The client emits usage metrics (&lt;code&gt;claude_code.token.usage&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;claude_code.cost.usage&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;claude_code.active_time.total&lt;/code&gt;) attributed to the authenticated developer’s identity: user ID, email, and group membership.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-8.png" alt="Amazon CloudWatch dashboard showing per-user token and cost metrics relayed from Claude Code sessions" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: OpenTelemetry metrics from Claude Code sessions relayed by the gateway and exported to Amazon CloudWatch by the collector&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway relays this telemetry over &lt;a href="https://opentelemetry.io/docs/specs/otel/protocol/"&gt;OpenTelemetry Protocol&lt;/a&gt; (OTLP) to a collector you configure. Supported OTLP-compatible backends include &lt;a href="https://www.datadoghq.com/"&gt;Datadog&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://www.splunk.com/"&gt;Splunk&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://grafana.com/"&gt;Grafana&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; through the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/otel/"&gt;AWS Distro for OpenTelemetry&lt;/a&gt; (ADOT) collector.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;telemetry:
  forward_to:
    - url: https://otel-collector.internal.example.com
      metrics: true
      logs: false
      traces: false&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Logs and traces are opt-in because they can contain source code and prompt content. Most deployments start with metrics only, which provide per-user cost and usage breakdowns without exposing sensitive data. For more information, refer to the Claude apps gateway &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-config" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;configuration&lt;/a&gt; page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="routing-inference-with-failover"&gt;4. Routing: Inference with failover&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway routes inference to one or more upstreams in declared order, failing over automatically on upstream unavailability, throttling, or timeouts. Cross-provider failover can change the applicable service terms and data-processing geography.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can configure combinations with the following upstream types:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;upstreams:
  # Amazon Bedrock (uses ECS task role, no static keys)
  - name: bedrock-east
    provider: bedrock
    region: us-east-1
    auth: {}
  # Amazon Bedrock in a second region for failover
  - name: bedrock-west
    provider: bedrock
    region: us-west-2
    auth: {}
  # Claude Platform on AWS (cross-provider fallback)
  - name: claude-platform
    provider: anthropicAws
    region: us-east-1
    workspace_id: wrkspc_01ABCDEFGHIJKLMN
    auth:
      api_key: ${ANTHROPIC_AWS_API_KEY}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;strong&gt;Deployment patterns&lt;/strong&gt; section shows how to combine these building blocks for common scenarios. For the full upstream configuration and provider-specific auth options, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-config#upstreams" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;upstreams reference&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="spend-caps-per-user-budget-enforcement"&gt;5. Spend caps: Per-user budget enforcement&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Budgets and AWS Cost Explorer provide account-level visibility with periodic aggregation, making them well suited for organizational cost governance. The gateway complements these tools by providing inline enforcement before inference occurs, in addition to visibility into per-developer usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Caps are set at three levels: organization-wide defaults, per-group, and per-user overrides. Each cap applies individually per developer, not as a shared pool. The gateway resolves the effective limit for each request: a per-user override takes precedence, then the most restrictive applicable group cap, then the organization default. If no cap exists at any level, spend is unlimited. When a developer reaches their ceiling, the gateway returns HTTP 429 immediately. Counters reset automatically at the start of each period (daily, weekly, or monthly).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# Org-wide default: $500/month per developer (amounts in USD cents)
curl -X POST https://&amp;lt;gateway&amp;gt;/v1/organizations/spend_limits \
  -H "x-api-key: $ADMIN_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"scope":{"type":"organization"},"amount":"50000","period":"monthly"}'

# Tighter cap for a specific group: $10/day for contractors
curl -X POST https://&amp;lt;gateway&amp;gt;/v1/organizations/spend_limits \
  -H "x-api-key: $ADMIN_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"scope":{"type":"rbac_group","rbac_group_id":"contractors"},"amount":"1000","period":"daily"}'

# Instant shutoff for one user: set cap to zero
curl -X POST https://&amp;lt;gateway&amp;gt;/v1/organizations/spend_limits \
  -H "x-api-key: $ADMIN_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"scope":{"type":"user","user_id":"&amp;lt;oidc-sub&amp;gt;"},"amount":"0","period":"daily"}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Spend caps are separate from model access control. A group may have access to Opus, Sonnet, and Haiku. Caps govern how much that access costs, not which models are available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Admin workflow: Caps are managed entirely through the Admin API. There is no admin UI. Platform teams typically automate this with a script that syncs limits from a checked-in config file as part of the deploy pipeline, or through Terraform calling the API. The &lt;code&gt;GET /v1/organizations/spend_limits/effective&lt;/code&gt; endpoint shows each developer’s resolved cap and period-to-date spend for reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Limitations to be aware of:&lt;/strong&gt; Spend is estimated from token counts at list price. It’s a real-time circuit breaker, not an invoice. Committed-use discounts and negotiated rates aren’t reflected. If the database is unavailable, spend enforcement fails open by default, allowing inference to continue. Organizations requiring strict budget enforcement can set &lt;code&gt;fail_closed_on_error: true&lt;/code&gt; to block requests instead. For authoritative billing, reconcile against Amazon Bedrock invocation logs or the AWS Cost and Usage Report. For the full Admin API reference and enforcement mechanics, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway-spend-limits" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;spend limits documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-9.png" alt="Claude Code showing a 429 error after the user reaches their daily spend limit" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: A user request is rejected with a 429 error on reaching the daily spend limit&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment-patterns"&gt;Deployment patterns&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How you deploy the gateway depends on your organization’s structure, traffic patterns, and governance requirements. There’s no single correct architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Centralizing all Claude usage through the gateway simplifies quota management and gives you one place for cost attribution and policy enforcement. Onboarding is typically instant: a new developer joins the identity provider group and gets access. The tradeoff is that the gateway becomes shared infrastructure your platform team operates, and workloads that need native Amazon Bedrock features cannot route through it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running Amazon Bedrock directly in dedicated accounts gives each team isolated quotas, no shared dependency, and access to the full Amazon Bedrock feature set. Direct Amazon Bedrock deployments can use IAM roles and retain centralized billing and audit data. The tradeoff is losing the gateway’s per-developer authentication, policy, telemetry, and spend controls.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most organizations combine both approaches in some form. The following patterns illustrate common configurations, from single-team setups to multi-account architectures. Start with the pattern closest to your current environment and evolve as your usage grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-a-single-team-single-region"&gt;Pattern A: Single team, single AWS Region&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended for:&lt;/strong&gt; teams evaluating the gateway or organizations with a single development group in one Region.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A minimal deployment: one Amazon Bedrock upstream in &lt;code&gt;us-east-1&lt;/code&gt;, an org-wide daily spend cap, and all developers get the same model access. Start here. Add complexity when the use case demands it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-10.png" alt="Diagram of a single team in one account and Region with an org-wide spend cap and one Amazon Bedrock upstream" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: Single team in a single account and Region, with org-wide daily, weekly, and monthly caps&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-b-multi-team-with-tiered-access"&gt;Pattern B: Multi-team with tiered access&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best for:&lt;/strong&gt; organizations with multiple teams that need different model access levels and spend limits. Groups from the identity provider drive differentiated policies:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Platform engineering:&lt;/strong&gt; Opus + Sonnet + Haiku, $50/day.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application developers:&lt;/strong&gt; Sonnet + Haiku, $20/day.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Contractors:&lt;/strong&gt; Haiku only, $5/day, web tools denied.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Group limits are inherited individually by each developer, not shared as a team budget. The Admin API reports spend per developer with group metadata, so team totals must be aggregated separately. The platform team can sync limits from a checked-in config file as part of the deploy pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-11.png" alt="Diagram of multiple teams in one account and Region with per-team spend caps driven by identity provider groups" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: Multiple teams in a single account and Region, with team-wide daily, weekly, and monthly caps&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-c-hybrid-bedrock-claude-platform-on-aws"&gt;Pattern C: Hybrid Amazon Bedrock + Claude Platform on AWS&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Best for:&lt;/strong&gt; organizations that want Amazon Bedrock as the preferred upstream with Claude Platform on AWS as overflow capacity. Note that cross-provider failover can change the applicable service terms and data-processing geography.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;upstreams:
  - name: claude-platform
    provider: anthropicAws
    region: us-east-1
    workspace_id: wrkspc_01ABCDEFGHIJKLMN
    auth:
      api_key: ${ANTHROPIC_AWS_API_KEY}
  - name: bedrock
    provider: bedrock
    region: us-east-1
    auth: {}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Requests go to Amazon Bedrock first. Only on rate-limit or outage does the gateway fall back to Claude Platform on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-12.png" alt="Diagram of a single account routing to Amazon Bedrock first and failing over to Claude Platform on AWS" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 12: Single account with multiple upstreams, using Amazon Bedrock and Claude Platform on AWS for failover&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-d-gateway-for-developer-tools-direct-bedrock-for-applications"&gt;Pattern D: Gateway for developer tools, direct Amazon Bedrock for applications&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended for:&lt;/strong&gt; organizations where developer tooling needs governance (SSO, spend caps, telemetry) but production applications call Amazon Bedrock directly with isolated quotas and native features.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-13.png" alt="Diagram separating governed developer tooling through the gateway from production applications that call Amazon Bedrock directly" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 13: Production workloads stay on dedicated Amazon Bedrock accounts with native features such as Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases, Agents, and Flows that the gateway does not proxy&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="pattern-e-multi-account-shared-services"&gt;Pattern E: Multi-account (shared services)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended for:&lt;/strong&gt; organizations where a central platform team operates the gateway and individual business units own their Amazon Bedrock access in separate accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;upstreams:
  - name: team-alpha
    provider: bedrock
    region: us-east-1
    auth:
      aws_access_key_id: ${TEAM_ALPHA_AKID}
      aws_secret_access_key: ${TEAM_ALPHA_SK}
  - name: team-beta
    provider: bedrock
    region: us-east-1
    auth:
      aws_access_key_id: ${TEAM_BETA_AKID}
      aws_secret_access_key: ${TEAM_BETA_SK}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Billing lands in each team’s account. The gateway routes to the correct upstream based on model configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-21574-14.png" alt="Diagram of a shared-services gateway routing to per-business-unit Amazon Bedrock accounts with separate billing" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 14: Multiple teams across multiple accounts, with per-account billing for Bedrock consumption&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Gateway lives in a shared-services account. Each business unit’s Amazon Bedrock usage is billed to their own AWS account. The gateway doesn’t natively assume a different IAM role per upstream. Multi-account routing requires explicit credentials in the upstream config. Store these credentials in AWS Secrets Manager and rotate them on a schedule. Long-lived access keys are a significant security and operational tradeoff. Consider an external process that periodically refreshes short-lived &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/STS/latest/APIReference/Welcome.html"&gt;AWS Security Token Service&lt;/a&gt; (AWS STS) credentials into the gateway’s environment to reduce exposure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Claude apps gateway gives platform teams a single control point for Claude Code and Claude Desktop on AWS. One container, one YAML configuration file. Five capabilities: SSO authentication, per-group model policies, per-user telemetry, multi-region routing with failover, and spend caps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There is no per-seat license fee. When using Amazon Bedrock as the upstream, no developer data leaves your AWS account. Developers run the same &lt;code&gt;claude&lt;/code&gt; binary they already know. The gateway is invisible to them after initial sign-in.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/anthropic-on-aws/tree/main/claude-apps-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; and choose one of two tracks. Both provision the same Amazon ECS Fargate deployment: an internal ALB, Amazon RDS for PostgreSQL, ECR, Secrets Manager, an IAM task role, and an ADOT telemetry collector. Choose the idempotent &lt;code&gt;setup.sh&lt;/code&gt; script for full visibility into every AWS call, or an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cdk/"&gt;AWS Cloud Development Kit&lt;/a&gt; (AWS CDK) stack for a managed lifecycle. For configuration details, refer to the &lt;a href="https://code.claude.com/docs/en/claude-apps-gateway"&gt;Claude apps gateway documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr&gt;
