		<item>
		<title>Run interactive IDEs on Amazon EKS with SageMaker AI to power up your AI workflows</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/run-interactive-ides-on-amazon-eks-with-sagemaker-ai-to-power-up-your-ai-workflows/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rajat Jain]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 10 Aug 2026 16:34:37 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0e2f8eb35e84867d4327e6e28f1d824f7b91a4a5</guid>

					<description>The Amazon SageMaker AI Spaces add-on for Amazon EKS runs managed JupyterLab and Code Editor environments on the cluster your ML team already operates. This post shows how to install and configure the add-on, connect from the browser and from VS Code over SSH-over-SSM, and move your team to OpenID Connect sign-in with Amazon Cognito.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;To power up AI workflows on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt;, data scientists need interactive IDEs like JupyterLab and Code Editor. Yet running those IDEs usually means leaving the cluster that hosts their pipelines, moving to a standalone JupyterHub deployment or a local laptop. That switch leaves them without the GPU nodes, shared storage, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; roles their pipelines depend on. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; Spaces add-on for Amazon EKS closes that gap. It runs managed JupyterLab and Code Editor environments on the cluster that you already operate. Standing up a standalone JupyterHub environment with GPU access, storage, and authentication typically takes a platform team 3–5 days. With the add-on, a data scientist launches a fully configured Space in about 5 minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you install the SageMaker AI Spaces add-on on an Amazon EKS cluster. You set up the supporting add-ons and IAM roles, deploy the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/aws-load-balancer-controller.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Load Balancer Controller&lt;/a&gt;, request a TLS certificate, and create an AWS Key Management System (AWS KMS) encryption key. You then create your first Space and reach it through a presigned URL in the browser and from VS Code over SSH-over-SSM. Finally, you review how to move your team to OpenID Connect (OIDC) sign-in with Amazon Cognito.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution runs on a single EKS cluster in three layers:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Network and access.&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/route53/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Route 53&lt;/a&gt; resolves a wildcard domain to an internet-facing Application Load Balancer (ALB) with TLS from &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/certificate-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)&lt;/a&gt;. For VS Code, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/systems-manager/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Systems Manager&lt;/a&gt; tunnels directly to the Space pod.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cluster routing.&lt;/strong&gt; The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/aws-load-balancer-controller.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Load Balancer Controller&lt;/a&gt; provisions the ALB. Traefik routes by hostname. Auth middleware validates tokens using &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)&lt;/a&gt; for JSON Web Token (JWT) encryption.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compute and storage.&lt;/strong&gt; Space pods run on private-subnet workers. The &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ebs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS)&lt;/a&gt; CSI driver provides persistent volumes, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/efs/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS)&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/fsx/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon FSx&lt;/a&gt; handle shared or high-throughput storage. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/pod-identities.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EKS Pod Identity&lt;/a&gt; grants pods scoped IAM roles.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consolidating interactive and training workloads on one cluster keeps GPU nodes busy between jobs. This can lift GPU utilization by up to 30 percent compared with a dedicated notebook fleet. It also avoids the cost of an always-on GPU environment, which can run into thousands of dollars a month.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-1.png" alt="Architecture routing Route 53 and an ALB through Traefik to SageMaker Space pods on an EKS cluster, with Pod Identity, AWS KMS, and Amazon EBS storage" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you need an AWS account with the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt; 2.x or later configured for your target AWS Region, plus &lt;a href="https://kubernetes.io/docs/tasks/tools/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;kubectl&lt;/a&gt; 1.30 or later and &lt;a href="https://helm.sh/docs/intro/install/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Helm v3&lt;/a&gt;. You also need a Route 53 public hosted zone for a domain you own, referenced as &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;YOUR_DOMAIN&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; throughout this post, and IAM permissions to create roles, policies, EKS add-ons, access entries, Pod Identity associations, ACM certificates, and KMS keys. The Spaces add-on must be version 0.1.4 or later, because earlier versions supported Amazon SageMaker HyperPod only.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-2.png" alt="Route 53 hosted zone showing the DNS validation CNAME records for the domain" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 2: Route 53 hosted zone with validation records&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set these variables once. The rest of the post reuses them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export CLUSTER_NAME=&amp;lt;CLUSTER_NAME&amp;gt;
export REGION=&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;
export ACCOUNT_ID=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every IAM role in this post is assumed by a Kubernetes service account through EKS Pod Identity, so they all share one trust policy. Save it once and reuse it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;cat &amp;gt; pod-identity-trust.json &amp;lt;&amp;lt;'EOF'
{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [{
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Principal": { "Service": "pods.eks.amazonaws.com" },
    "Action": ["sts:AssumeRole", "sts:TagSession"]
  }]
}
EOF&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; This walkthrough creates resources that incur AWS charges: an internet-facing ALB, EBS volumes, and an EKS cluster. SSM advanced-instances tier adds about $0.00695/hr per Space pod. Follow the &lt;a href="#cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/a&gt; section when you finish.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-the-eks-cluster"&gt;Create the EKS cluster&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cluster creation itself follows the standard &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/eks/latest/userguide/getting-started-console.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EKS getting started guide&lt;/a&gt;. What matters here is meeting four Spaces-specific requirements. Keep &lt;strong&gt;EKS Auto Mode&lt;/strong&gt; disabled, because the add-on requires classic EC2-backed nodes on Kubernetes 1.30 or later. Use a virtual private cloud (VPC) with public and private subnets across at least two Availability Zones, with a NAT gateway serving the private subnets, and set cluster endpoint access to &lt;strong&gt;Public and private&lt;/strong&gt;. During creation, add the &lt;strong&gt;EKS Pod Identity Agent&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Amazon EBS CSI Driver&lt;/strong&gt;, &lt;strong&gt;Cert manager&lt;/strong&gt;, and &lt;strong&gt;External DNS&lt;/strong&gt; add-ons, but hold off on Amazon SageMaker Spaces and the AWS Load Balancer Controller. You install those later. Finally, create a managed node group on your private subnets with Amazon Linux 2023, &lt;code&gt;m5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; or larger, and 2 nodes. Skip ahead if you already run a cluster that fits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One step is often overlooked. Tag every subnet in the VPC so the AWS Load Balancer Controller can discover them, and tag them &lt;em&gt;before&lt;/em&gt; you install the Spaces add-on. Otherwise, the controller can place the ALB on private subnets, making Spaces unreachable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;export VPC_ID=$(aws eks describe-cluster \
  --name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --query 'cluster.resourcesVpcConfig.vpcId' --output text)

ALL_SUBNETS=$(aws ec2 describe-subnets --region $REGION \
  --filters "Name=vpc-id,Values=${VPC_ID}" \
  --query 'Subnets[*].SubnetId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-tags --region $REGION --resources ${ALL_SUBNETS} \
  --tags Key=kubernetes.io/cluster/$CLUSTER_NAME,Value=shared

PUBLIC_SUBNETS=$(aws ec2 describe-subnets --region $REGION \
  --filters "Name=vpc-id,Values=${VPC_ID}" "Name=map-public-ip-on-launch,Values=true" \
  --query 'Subnets[*].SubnetId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-tags --region $REGION --resources ${PUBLIC_SUBNETS} \
  --tags Key=kubernetes.io/role/elb,Value=1

PRIVATE_SUBNETS=$(aws ec2 describe-subnets --region $REGION \
  --filters "Name=vpc-id,Values=${VPC_ID}" "Name=map-public-ip-on-launch,Values=false" \
  --query 'Subnets[*].SubnetId' --output text)
aws ec2 create-tags --region $REGION --resources ${PRIVATE_SUBNETS} \
  --tags Key=kubernetes.io/role/internal-elb,Value=1&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="set-up-the-foundation"&gt;Set up the foundation&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the cluster running, you point kubectl at it, confirm the add-on pods are healthy, and give External DNS the Route 53 permissions that it needs to manage DNS records.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure kubectl: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks update-kubeconfig --name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION
kubectl get nodes&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Both workers report &lt;code&gt;Ready&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; &lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;NAME                        STATUS   ROLES    AGE   VERSION
ip-10-0-1-42.ec2.internal   Ready    &amp;lt;none&amp;gt;   38m   v1.34.6-eks-bbe087e
ip-10-0-2-96.ec2.internal   Ready    &amp;lt;none&amp;gt;   38m   v1.34.6-eks-bbe087e&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the system pods are healthy across the add-on namespaces with &lt;code&gt;kubectl get pods -A&lt;/code&gt;. Every pod in &lt;code&gt;kube-system&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;cert-manager&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;external-dns&lt;/code&gt; should be &lt;code&gt;Running&lt;/code&gt; before you continue.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;External DNS needs Route 53 permissions to manage records. Create the role, attach a least-privilege policy, and bind it through Pod Identity: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws iam create-role --role-name ExternalDNSRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://pod-identity-trust.json

aws iam put-role-policy --role-name ExternalDNSRole \
  --policy-name ExternalDNSRoute53Policy \
  --policy-document '{
  "Version":"2012-10-17",
  "Statement":[
    {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["route53:ChangeResourceRecordSets"],
     "Resource":"arn:aws:route53:::hostedzone/*"},
    {"Effect":"Allow","Action":["route53:ListHostedZones","route53:ListResourceRecordSets","route53:ListTagsForResource"],
     "Resource":"*"}
  ]}'

aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
  --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --namespace external-dns --service-account external-dns \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::${ACCOUNT_ID}:role/ExternalDNSRole

kubectl rollout restart deployment -n external-dns external-dns&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security note:&lt;/strong&gt; Scope each Pod Identity role to minimum actions and resources. Prefer explicit resource ARNs over wildcards, and confirm only the intended service account can assume the role.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="install-the-aws-load-balancer-controller"&gt;Install the AWS Load Balancer Controller&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AWS Load Balancer Controller provisions the ALB that fronts your Spaces UI. Install it with Helm.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define the controller’s IAM policy, role, and Pod Identity association: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -sS -o /tmp/lbc-iam-policy.json \
  &lt;a href="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kubernetes-sigs/aws-load-balancer-controller/main/docs/install/iam_policy.json" rel="noopener noreferrer"&gt;https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kubernetes-sigs/aws-load-balancer-controller/main/docs/install/iam_policy.json&lt;/a&gt;

aws iam create-policy --policy-name AWSLoadBalancerControllerIAMPolicy \
  --policy-document file:///tmp/lbc-iam-policy.json

aws iam create-role --role-name AWSLoadBalancerControllerRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://pod-identity-trust.json

aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name AWSLoadBalancerControllerRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::${ACCOUNT_ID}:policy/AWSLoadBalancerControllerIAMPolicy

aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
  --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --namespace kube-system --service-account aws-load-balancer-controller \
  --role-arn arn:aws:iam::${ACCOUNT_ID}:role/AWSLoadBalancerControllerRole&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Install the Helm chart. Pass &lt;code&gt;vpcId&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;region&lt;/code&gt; explicitly. On chart v3.2+, the controller fails if it auto-detects the VPC through EC2 metadata, which EKS blocks for pods. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;helm repo add eks https://aws.github.io/eks-charts
helm repo update eks

helm install aws-load-balancer-controller eks/aws-load-balancer-controller \
  -n kube-system \
  --set clusterName=$CLUSTER_NAME \
  --set serviceAccount.create=true \
  --set serviceAccount.name=aws-load-balancer-controller \
  --set region=$REGION \
  --set vpcId=$VPC_ID

kubectl rollout status deployment -n kube-system aws-load-balancer-controller --timeout=180s&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Both controller replicas come up:&lt;/p&gt; &lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;NAME                           READY   UP-TO-DATE   AVAILABLE   AGE
aws-load-balancer-controller   2/2     2            2           174m&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="create-the-certificate-key-and-ssm-configuration"&gt;Create the certificate, key, and SSM configuration&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Spaces add-on needs a TLS certificate, a KMS key for JWT encryption, and SSM service settings for remote access.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Request an ACM certificate covering your domain and a wildcard, using DNS validation, then read back the CNAME records ACM expects: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;CERT_ARN=$(aws acm request-certificate \
  --domain-name "&amp;lt;YOUR_DOMAIN&amp;gt;" \
  --subject-alternative-names "*.&amp;lt;YOUR_DOMAIN&amp;gt;" \
  --validation-method DNS \
  --region $REGION \
  --query CertificateArn --output text)

# Read the CNAME records ACM expects, then add them to your Route 53
# hosted zone. The console's 'Create records in Route 53' button
# does this for you.
aws acm describe-certificate --certificate-arn "$CERT_ARN" \
  --region $REGION \
  --query 'Certificate.DomainValidationOptions[].ResourceRecord'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Wait for the certificate status to reach &lt;strong&gt;Issued&lt;/strong&gt;, then copy the ARN.&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-3.png" alt="ACM console showing an issued certificate for the domain and its wildcard subdomain" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 3: Certificate issued for the domain&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;blockquote&gt;
   &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security note:&lt;/strong&gt; DNS validation verifies domain ownership and triggers ACM automatic renewal. Keep the validation CNAMEs in Route 53. Removing them breaks renewal.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/blockquote&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a KMS encryption key. The auth middleware calls &lt;code&gt;kms:GenerateDataKey&lt;/code&gt; per JWT, so the key must be symmetric &lt;code&gt;ENCRYPT_DECRYPT&lt;/code&gt;, which is the CLI default: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;KMS_KEY_ARN=$(aws kms create-key --region $REGION \
  --description "SageMaker Spaces JWT encryption" \
  --query 'KeyMetadata.Arn' --output text)

aws kms create-alias --region $REGION \
  --alias-name alias/sagemaker-spaces-jwt \
  --target-key-id "$KMS_KEY_ARN"&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Turn on the SSM advanced-instances tier. Session Manager tunnels to hybrid managed instances, which is what VS Code remote uses, require this tier (about $0.00695/hr per Space pod): 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws ssm update-service-setting --region $REGION \
  --setting-id arn:aws:ssm:$REGION:${ACCOUNT_ID}:servicesetting/ssm/managed-instance/activation-tier \
  --setting-value advanced&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="install-the-spaces-add-on"&gt;Install the Spaces add-on&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You create IAM roles for the Spaces controller and auth middleware, then install the add-on.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Start with the SSM managed-instance role that each Space pod uses in the SSM fleet: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws iam create-role --role-name SageMakerSpacesSSMManagedNodeRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document '{
  "Version":"2012-10-17",
  "Statement":[{"Effect":"Allow","Principal":{"Service":"ssm.amazonaws.com"},"Action":"sts:AssumeRole"}]
}'

aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name SageMakerSpacesSSMManagedNodeRole \
  --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/AmazonSSMManagedInstanceCore&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Next, create the Spaces controller role. It needs SSM, PassRole, and KMS permissions. Save the following policy as &lt;code&gt;spaces-controller-policy.json&lt;/code&gt;, replacing &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;KMS_KEY_ARN&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt; with your own values: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "SSMAccountLevel",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "ssm:CreateActivation",
        "ssm:DeleteActivation",
        "ssm:DescribeActivations",
        "ssm:DescribeInstanceInformation",
        "ssm:DeregisterManagedInstance",
        "ssm:ListTagsForResource",
        "ssm:AddTagsToResource",
        "ssm:ListDocuments",
        "ssm:DescribeSessions"
      ],
      "Resource": "*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "SSMDocumentMgmt",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "ssm:CreateDocument",
        "ssm:DescribeDocument",
        "ssm:GetDocument",
        "ssm:UpdateDocument",
        "ssm:UpdateDocumentDefaultVersion",
        "ssm:DeleteDocument"
      ],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:ssm:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:document/SageMaker-Space*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "SSMSessionMgmt",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "ssm:StartSession",
        "ssm:TerminateSession",
        "ssm:ResumeSession",
        "ssm:GetConnectionStatus"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:ssm:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:document/SageMaker-Space*",
        "arn:aws:ssm:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:managed-instance/*",
        "arn:aws:ssm:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;::document/AWS-StartSSHSession"
      ]
    },
    {
      "Sid": "PassSSMManagedNodeRole",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "iam:PassRole",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:iam::&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:role/SageMakerSpacesSSMManagedNodeRole",
      "Condition": {
        "StringEquals": {
          "iam:PassedToService": "ssm.amazonaws.com"
        }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "KMSForJWT",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": [
        "kms:GenerateDataKey",
        "kms:Decrypt",
        "kms:Encrypt",
        "kms:DescribeKey"
      ],
      "Resource": "&amp;lt;KMS_KEY_ARN&amp;gt;"
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Create the role and attach the policy:&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws iam create-role --role-name SageMakerSpacesControllerRole \
  --assume-role-policy-document file://pod-identity-trust.json

aws iam put-role-policy --role-name SageMakerSpacesControllerRole \
  --policy-name SageMakerSpacesControllerPolicy \
  --policy-document file://spaces-controller-policy.json&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Bind controller and auth middleware service accounts to this role through Pod Identity: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;for SA in jupyter-k8s-controller-manager jupyter-k8s-authmiddleware; do
  aws eks create-pod-identity-association \
    --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
    --namespace jupyter-k8s-system --service-account $SA \
    --role-arn arn:aws:iam::${ACCOUNT_ID}:role/SageMakerSpacesControllerRole
done&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;blockquote&gt;
   &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security note:&lt;/strong&gt; For tighter separation of duties, split this into two roles: one with SSM actions for the controller, and one with KMS encrypt and decrypt for the auth middleware.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/blockquote&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define &lt;code&gt;addon-config.yaml&lt;/code&gt; with your domain, certificate ARN, key ARN, and managed-node role name: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;jupyter-k8s:
  # 'enable' (not 'enabled') is correct here, per the official AWS docs:
  # https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/operator-install.html
  # The jupyter-k8s and jupyter-k8s-aws-hyperpod subcharts use different
  # schemas, so clusterWebUI below correctly uses 'enabled'. Not a typo.
  workspacePodWatching:
    enable: true
jupyter-k8s-aws-hyperpod:
  clusterWebUI:
    enabled: true
    domain: "&amp;lt;YOUR_DOMAIN&amp;gt;"
    awsCertificateArn: "&amp;lt;ACM_CERT_ARN&amp;gt;"
  traefik:
    shouldInstall: true
  auth:
    kmsKeyId: "&amp;lt;KMS_KEY_ARN&amp;gt;"
  remoteAccess:
    enabled: true
    ssmManagedNodeRole: SageMakerSpacesSSMManagedNodeRole&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Install the add-on: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks create-addon \
  --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-spaces \
  --configuration-values file://addon-config.yaml \
  --resolve-conflicts OVERWRITE&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Poll until the add-on reaches &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; (about three minutes): 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks describe-addon \
  --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-spaces \
  --query 'addon.{status:status,version:addonVersion,issues:health.issues}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;The add-on reports &lt;code&gt;ACTIVE&lt;/code&gt; with an empty &lt;code&gt;issues&lt;/code&gt; list:&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "status": "ACTIVE",
  "version": "v0.1.4-eksbuild.1",
  "issues": []
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm all Spaces pods are &lt;code&gt;Running&lt;/code&gt;: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get pods -n jupyter-k8s-system&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;The controller, two auth middleware replicas, and two Traefik routers should all be &lt;code&gt;Running&lt;/code&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; &lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;NAME                                          READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
jupyter-k8s-controller-manager-65fcd4d67f-*   1/1     Running   0          3h13m
workspace-auth-middleware-c7f7fbb6d-*         1/1     Running   0          3h13m
workspace-auth-middleware-c7f7fbb6d-*         1/1     Running   0          3h13m
workspace-traefik-router-755d494fbf-*         1/1     Running   0          3h13m
workspace-traefik-router-755d494fbf-*         1/1     Running   0          3h13m&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="grant-user-access-and-create-a-space"&gt;Grant user access and create a Space&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the add-on healthy, you grant a user access to the cluster and create the first JupyterLab Space. Access relies on an EKS access entry scoped to a single namespace, so users can’t reach resources outside it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Grant access through an EKS access entry. In the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;EKS console&lt;/a&gt;, navigate to your cluster’s &lt;strong&gt;Access&lt;/strong&gt; tab and choose &lt;strong&gt;Create access entry&lt;/strong&gt;. Choose your IAM user or role, then add &lt;code&gt;AmazonSagemakerHyperpodSpacePolicy&lt;/code&gt; for the &lt;code&gt;default&lt;/code&gt; namespace. 
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-4.png" alt="EKS console Access tab creating an access entry scoped to the default namespace" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 4: Access entry for namespace&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;blockquote&gt;
   &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security note:&lt;/strong&gt; Prefer namespace-scoped access over cluster-wide policies so users can’t modify resources outside their namespace.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/blockquote&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;List the pre-installed Workspace templates and access strategies: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl get workspacetemplate -A
kubectl get workspaceaccessstrategies -A&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;You see &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-jupyter-template&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-code-editor-template&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;hyperpod-access-strategy&lt;/code&gt; in &lt;code&gt;jupyter-k8s-system&lt;/code&gt;. Reference these in your Workspace rather than repeating configuration inline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Define &lt;code&gt;workspace.yaml&lt;/code&gt; for a JupyterLab Space: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;apiVersion: workspace.jupyter.org/v1alpha1
kind: Workspace
metadata:
  name: my-space
  namespace: default
spec:
  templateRef:
    name: sagemaker-jupyter-template
    namespace: jupyter-k8s-system
  appType: jupyterlab
  accessType: OwnerOnly
  accessStrategy:
    name: hyperpod-access-strategy
    namespace: jupyter-k8s-system
  image: public.ecr.aws/sagemaker/sagemaker-distribution:latest-cpu&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;accessType: OwnerOnly&lt;/code&gt; restricts browser access to the IAM principal that created the Space. Use &lt;code&gt;Public&lt;/code&gt; for any namespace-authorized user.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Apply and wait for the Space to become Available: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl apply -f workspace.yaml
kubectl get workspace -n default -w&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;pre class="text"&gt;&lt;code&gt;workspace.workspace.jupyter.org/my-space created&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; &lt;p&gt;First-time startup takes about five minutes. The cluster pulls the 4 GB SageMaker Distribution image and registers the pod with SSM.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-5.png" alt="The my-space Workspace reporting an Available status" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Space running&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="connect-in-the-browser"&gt;Connect in the browser&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Spaces controller issues a short-lived, presigned URL that carries the user’s encrypted token. You generate one, then navigate to it in your browser.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Generate a short-lived presigned URL: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl create -f - -o yaml &amp;lt;&amp;lt;EOF
apiVersion: connection.workspace.jupyter.org/v1alpha1
kind: WorkspaceConnection
metadata:
  namespace: default
  generateName: my-space-conn-
spec:
  workspaceName: my-space
  workspaceConnectionType: web-ui
EOF&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Find &lt;code&gt;status.workspaceConnectionUrl&lt;/code&gt; in the response and navigate to the URL in your browser:&lt;/p&gt; 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;status:
  workspaceConnectionType: web-ui
  workspaceConnectionUrl: https://my-space-&amp;lt;id&amp;gt;.&amp;lt;YOUR_DOMAIN&amp;gt;/bearer-auth?token=eyJhbGciOiJIUzM4NCIsImVkayI6...&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; 
  &lt;blockquote&gt;
   &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Security note:&lt;/strong&gt; Presigned URLs carry the user’s KMS-encrypted JWT with a 5-minute expiry enforced by the &lt;code&gt;exp&lt;/code&gt; claim. This value isn’t configurable in the current add-on version. Don’t log or share presigned URLs over unencrypted channels. For durable access, use VS Code remote.&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/blockquote&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-6.png" alt="JupyterLab running in the browser at the custom Spaces domain" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 6: JupyterLab on the custom domain&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="connect-from-vs-code"&gt;Connect from VS Code&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a local IDE experience, VS Code connects to the Space pod through an SSM tunnel, with no browser, domain, or ALB required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Install &lt;a href="https://code.visualstudio.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;VS Code&lt;/a&gt;, the &lt;a href="https://marketplace.visualstudio.com/items?itemName=AmazonWebServices.aws-toolkit-vscode" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Toolkit extension&lt;/a&gt;, and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/systems-manager/latest/userguide/session-manager-working-with-install-plugin.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Session Manager plugin&lt;/a&gt; locally.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Generate a VS Code connection URL by creating the same WorkspaceConnection resource as before, with &lt;code&gt;workspaceConnectionType: vscode-remote&lt;/code&gt; instead of &lt;code&gt;web-ui&lt;/code&gt;. This time the response carries a &lt;code&gt;vscode://&lt;/code&gt; deep link instead of an HTTPS URL: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;status:
  workspaceConnectionType: vscode-remote
  workspaceConnectionUrl: vscode://amazonwebservices.aws-toolkit-vscode/connect/workspace?sessionId=eks-Sagemaker--jupyter-k8s-...&amp;amp;sessionToken=...&amp;amp;streamUrl=wss://ssmmessages.&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;.amazonaws.com/v1/data-channel/...&amp;amp;workspaceName=my-space&amp;amp;namespace=default&amp;amp;eksClusterArn=arn:aws:eks:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:cluster/&amp;lt;CLUSTER_NAME&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Paste the &lt;code&gt;vscode://&lt;/code&gt; URL into your browser. The browser prompts you to open the link in VS Code. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-7.png" alt="Browser prompt asking to open the vscode:// link in VS Code" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 7: Browser opens VS Code&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Accept the prompt. AWS Toolkit establishes an SSH-over-SSM tunnel to the Space, and VS Code attaches to the remote filesystem. &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-8.png" alt="VS Code connected to the remote Space filesystem over an SSM tunnel" width="800"&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 8: VS Code with remote kernel&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For private-subnet configurations and SDK alternatives, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/vscode-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Remote access to SageMaker AI Spaces&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="sign-in-with-corporate-credentials-using-oidc"&gt;Sign in with corporate credentials using OIDC&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Access so far relies on IAM users and roles. To let your team sign in with corporate credentials instead, register an OIDC provider with the cluster and bind Kubernetes role-based access control (RBAC) to identity provider groups. Kubernetes then authorizes people by group membership, with no IAM principal per user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The open source &lt;a href="https://jupyter-deploy.readthedocs.io/en/latest/templates/aws-eks-oidc-template/index.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;jupyter-deploy&lt;/a&gt; project ships an &lt;code&gt;aws-eks-oidc&lt;/code&gt; template that sets this up for you. &lt;a href="https://dexidp.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Dex&lt;/a&gt; runs in the cluster as the OIDC provider, Amazon EKS trusts it as an identity provider, and a web console gives your team self-service workspace management. The template provisions its own VPC and cluster, so run it alongside the cluster from this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-9.png" alt="Architecture of self-managed OIDC sign-in using Dex and Amazon Cognito with Amazon EKS" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 9: Self-managed OIDC with Amazon Cognito&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The template ships a Dex connector for GitHub. Amazon Cognito works through the generic &lt;code&gt;oidc&lt;/code&gt; connector in Dex instead, and needs two claim mappings that GitHub never requires. Amazon EKS reads the username from the &lt;code&gt;preferred_username&lt;/code&gt; claim, which Cognito doesn’t issue, so map it from &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt;. Cognito also publishes group membership as &lt;code&gt;cognito:groups&lt;/code&gt; rather than &lt;code&gt;groups&lt;/code&gt;. Miss the username mapping and requests reach the API server with no resolvable user, and the console reports an expired session rather than an authorization error. The template binds its RBAC role to a group named &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;org&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;team&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;, so create a Cognito group with that exact name and add your users to it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your team then signs in at the Cognito managed login page. With a single connector configured, Dex skips the provider chooser.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-10.png" alt="Amazon Cognito managed login page for signing in to the workspace console" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 10: Cognito managed login&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The console lists and creates workspaces under that identity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-12.png" alt="JupyterLab launched and authorized as the signed-in Amazon Cognito user" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 11: Self-service workspace management&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Opening one launches JupyterLab, authorized as the Cognito user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img loading="lazy" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/06/ML-20787-11.png" alt="Web console listing workspaces for self-service management under the signed-in identity" width="800" height="998"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 12: JupyterLab for the Cognito user&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete resources in reverse order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the Space and the add-on: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl delete workspace my-space -n default

aws eks delete-addon --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION \
  --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-spaces&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Uninstall the Load Balancer Controller and remaining add-ons: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;helm uninstall aws-load-balancer-controller -n kube-system

for a in aws-ebs-csi-driver external-dns cert-manager eks-pod-identity-agent kube-proxy; do
  aws eks delete-addon --cluster-name $CLUSTER_NAME --region $REGION --addon-name $a
done&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the IAM roles, policies, and Pod Identity associations you created (&lt;code&gt;ExternalDNSRole&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;AWSLoadBalancerControllerRole&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;AWSLoadBalancerControllerIAMPolicy&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;SageMakerSpacesControllerRole&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;SageMakerSpacesSSMManagedNodeRole&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the certificate, schedule the KMS key for deletion (7-day minimum), and remove the Route 53 records.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Revert the SSM advanced-instances tier to stop per-instance charges across the account: 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws ssm update-service-setting --region $REGION \
  --setting-id arn:aws:ssm:$REGION:${ACCOUNT_ID}:servicesetting/ssm/managed-instance/activation-tier \
  --setting-value standard&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the node group and EKS cluster, and delete the VPC if you created it for this walkthrough.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Skipping these steps continues to incur charges for the EKS cluster, node group, EBS volumes, ALB, and each registered hybrid instance on advanced tier.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you installed the SageMaker AI Spaces add-on on an Amazon EKS cluster and configured browser and VS Code access. You also saw how to move your team to OIDC sign-in with Amazon Cognito. By consolidating interactive IDEs onto the cluster you already run, you manage one environment instead of two and cut time-to-first-notebook from days to minutes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To go further, attach AWS WAF, federate additional providers, split controller and auth middleware IAM roles, or set namespace-level resource quotas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For related approaches, see:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/power-up-your-ml-workflows-with-interactive-ides-on-sagemaker-hyperpod/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Power up your ML workflows with interactive IDEs on SageMaker HyperPod&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerate-foundation-model-training-and-inference-with-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-and-amazon-sagemaker-studio/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Accelerate foundation model training and inference with Amazon SageMaker HyperPod and Amazon SageMaker Studio&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How nOps shipped FinOps agents 75% faster with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-nops-shipped-finops-agents-75-faster-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Jordan Stein]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 10 Aug 2026 16:30:46 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2a9a1aff9c94f081507dacb658dfe9013b40dd70</guid>

					<description>nOps rebuilt its Clara FinOps AI agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, replacing a self-managed Amazon EKS stack running LangChain and LangGraph. The move cut time-to-production by 75% (from 10-12 months to 4 months), improved response quality, and reduced operational overhead while keeping analytics governed through Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://nops.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;nOps&lt;/a&gt;, an AI-powered cloud optimization solution, recently reimagined its Financial Operations (FinOps) analytics capabilities by transitioning to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a service to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. The new foundation helps nOps better serve customers managing commitment optimization across Amazon Web Services (AWS), Google Cloud Platform (GCP), and Microsoft Azure. Through continual optimization of commitments such as Reserved Instances and AWS Savings Plans, nOps helps teams maximize savings, reduce risk, and automate away the operational burden of manual FinOps. Today, nOps supports customers representing more than USD $4 billion in cloud spend under management.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explain how nOps transitioned our analytics and agent experience to accelerate product delivery, improve response quality, and reduce operational complexity using Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views, Databricks Lakebase, Amazon DynamoDB, and Vercel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scaling-finops-ai-beyond-the-limits-of-api-centric-infrastructure"&gt;Scaling FinOps AI beyond the limits of API-centric infrastructure&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As we expanded our product portfolio and customer base, the infrastructure, front-end, and back-end teams needed to support increasingly complex analytics workflows while maintaining high reliability and multi-tenant isolation. Existing infrastructure patterns introduced friction:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Response latency and inconsistency:&lt;/strong&gt; Long context messages from API-based data access increased latency and reduced response consistency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;System complexity:&lt;/strong&gt; Multiple orchestration and observability layers increased system complexity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Innovation drag:&lt;/strong&gt; Engineering time was increasingly consumed by infrastructure maintenance rather than product innovation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“We were attempting to build advanced AI capabilities on top of infrastructure that wasn’t designed for analytics-driven agents, which made iteration slow, complex, and prone to inaccuracies.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Jordan Stein&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="current-state"&gt;Current state&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before transitioning, we introduced Clara, our FinOps AI agent, on top of existing infrastructure components including Kubernetes, Amazon Bedrock model invocation, LangChain/LangGraph orchestration, and tool wrappers around web APIs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This allowed rapid initial delivery, but it also revealed structural limits:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Slower time to insight&lt;/strong&gt;: API-shaped data and large context windows increased turn latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Operational overhead&lt;/strong&gt;: Multiple non-purpose-built layers made iteration and debugging harder.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data path mismatch&lt;/strong&gt;: Agent answers were tied to API responses instead of a dedicated semantic analytics layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To address this, we shifted to a purpose-built architecture centered on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore for runtime/orchestration, Databricks Metric Views for governed analytics semantics, and Databricks Lakebase for durable application state. We chose Bedrock AgentCore for its managed agent runtime, built-in memory, and orchestration, along with the freedom to use any framework or model. With this freedom, we built on Strands and evolved our model choices without switching services, so the team could focus on domain logic rather than infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We designed the new architecture to scale with demand, produce accurate answers, and allow developers to ship faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the end-to-end architecture of the nOps Clara solution built on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/07/31/ML-20862-1.png" alt="Architecture of the nOps Clara solution: a Vercel web application, the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore agent runtime, a Databricks Lakehouse data layer, and an async workflow layer using Amazon DynamoDB, Amazon SNS, Amazon SQS, and Amazon API Gateway." width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end architecture of the nOps Clara solution on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution consists of:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Interaction layer:&lt;/strong&gt; A Vercel-hosted Next.js web application where customers interact with Clara through a conversational interface backed by streaming Server-Sent Events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent runtime layer:&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock AgentCore running a single Strands-based agent with direct tool access for canvas operations, query execution, datasource discovery, and workflow orchestration, which alleviates multi-agent routing overhead.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data layer:&lt;/strong&gt; Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views providing a governed semantic analytics layer, with Databricks Lakebase (serverless PostgreSQL) storing durable application state such as sessions, canvases, and widget specifications.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Async workflow layer:&lt;/strong&gt; An event-driven execution path using Amazon DynamoDB job tracking, Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS) and Amazon Simple Queue Service (Amazon SQS) notifications, and Amazon API Gateway WebSocket push for long-running analytics tasks that update the UI in real time.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="web-application"&gt;Web application&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the interaction layer, customers use Clara through a Vercel-hosted web application. Requests flow through a Next.js Backend for Frontend (BFF) that invokes Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Clara runs as a Strands-based agent runtime that supports streaming responses and tool calling. &lt;strong&gt;Users can invoke the same workflows manually that Clara calls through Strands tools.&lt;/strong&gt; This is an important requirement and building block for Clara because it requires the agent to perform tasks that follow the same procedures as a human would within the product requirements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-bedrock-agentcore-backend"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore backend&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The runtime is deployed as a Docker container on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, with the full infrastructure (runtime, memory, guardrails, queues, and worker functions) defined in a single AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) stack. Clara uses a single-agent architecture rather than a multi-agent router: one Strands Agent with direct access to the following tools: canvas operations, query execution, datasource discovery, and workflow orchestration. This avoids the latency and error-propagation overhead of agent-to-agent handoffs while keeping tool dispatch deterministic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Streaming is central to the experience. A custom merge layer sits between the Strands async stream and the Server-Sent Event output, handling three concerns in one pass:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Heartbeats keep connections alive during long tool executions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A text buffer with word-boundary-aware flushing coalesces small model deltas into readable chunks to help prevent user interface (UI) flicker.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A widget-poll worker interleaves real-time canvas update events into the same stream.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Clara uses &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/memory.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore memory&lt;/a&gt; with three strategies (semantic facts, user preferences, and canvas summaries), so it learns how each user engages with their data over time. Preferences inform layout choices, default aggregations, and chart types. Facts capture organizational context such as account structures and cost allocation conventions. Canvas summaries preserve the analytical thread across sessions so users can pick up where they left off without re-explaining context. Sessions are scoped by canvas (not the ephemeral HTTP session), so conversation context persists across browser refreshes and reconnections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For tenant isolation, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; runs as a standalone pre-check on the raw user prompt before the agent is invoked, enforcing cross-tenant data access policies and prompt-attack detection. A tenant policy layer then sanitizes outbound stream chunks and widget events, redacting internal identifiers before they reach the front end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="data-lakehouse"&gt;Data lakehouse&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the data layer, Clara no longer depends on generic product APIs for analytical context. Instead, it uses curated tools to execute Structured Query Language (SQL) against Databricks SQL Warehouse backed by &lt;a href="https://docs.databricks.com/aws/en/business-semantics/metric-views" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views&lt;/a&gt;. This provides a governed semantic layer for measures and dimensions, improving consistency between conversational answers and dashboard outputs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="example"&gt;Example&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Metric views compared to raw SQL (True Customer Cost)&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;User asks: “Show my true AWS Cost for the last 30 days by account.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Raw SQL Model Context Protocol (MCP) approach:&lt;/strong&gt; The tool must recalculate business logic every time across various AWS pricing programs and commitment-based discounts, then join and normalize those values before grouping by account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Raw SQL MCP approach
-- User asks: "True Customer Cost for last 30 days by account"
SELECT
    a.account_name,
    SUM(
        -- Base amortized cost
        COALESCE(c.amortized_cost, 0)
        -- Apply EDP discount
        - COALESCE(c.edp_discount_amount, 0)
        -- Apply PPA credits
        - COALESCE(c.ppa_credit_amount, 0)
        -- Add RI effective amortized component
        + COALESCE(c.ri_effective_cost, 0)
        -- Add Savings Plan effective amortized component
        + COALESCE(c.sp_effective_cost, 0)
    ) AS true_customer_cost
FROM raw_cost_and_usage c
JOIN account_dim a
    ON c.account_id = a.account_id
LEFT JOIN pricing_adjustments p
    ON c.account_id = p.account_id
    AND c.usage_day = p.usage_day
WHERE c.usage_day &amp;gt;= date_sub(current_date(), 30)
    AND c.usage_day &amp;lt; current_date()
    AND c.charge_type NOT IN ('Credit', 'Refund')
GROUP BY a.account_name
ORDER BY true_customer_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric View MCP approach:&lt;/strong&gt; The tool queries a predefined measure, utilizing detailed comments and synonyms, (for example, &lt;code&gt;true_customer_cost&lt;/code&gt;) with a dimension (&lt;code&gt;account_name&lt;/code&gt;) and a time range (last 30 days).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;-- Metric View MCP approach
-- Same user ask, but financial logic is pre-modeled in metric view measure
SELECT
    account_name,
    SUM(true_customer_cost) AS true_customer_cost
FROM mv_true_customer_cost
WHERE usage_day &amp;gt;= date_sub(current_date(), 30)
    AND usage_day &amp;lt; current_date()
GROUP BY account_name
ORDER BY true_customer_cost DESC;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result:&lt;/strong&gt; One governed definition of True Customer Cost, simpler MCP logic, and consistent outputs across chat and dashboards.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How a Metric View measure appears to a large language model (LLM)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;ID:&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;code&gt;true_customer_cost&lt;/code&gt;&lt;br&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Display Name:&lt;/strong&gt; True Customer Cost&lt;br&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Comment:&lt;/strong&gt; The true cost of a customer’s spend after taking credits, refunds, commitments, and discounts, such as Enterprise Discount Program (EDP) and Private Pricing Agreements (PPA).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Synonyms:&lt;/strong&gt; Custom fields to be used for LLM analysis. We have co-opted Synonyms for use with key:value pairs to send additional metadata to the front end.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="state-layer"&gt;State layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At the state layer, Databricks Lakebase (serverless PostgreSQL) stores durable product objects, sessions, canvases, widgets, and query/chart specifications, so insights generated in chat can be promoted into persistent, shareable analytics artifacts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="analytics-workflow"&gt;Analytics workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For long-running analytics workflows, we introduced an asynchronous execution path using workflow workers, Amazon DynamoDB job tracking, Amazon SNS and Amazon SQS notifications, and Amazon API Gateway WebSocket push. Clara can then provide responsive user interactions while heavy processing completes in the background and updates the UI in real time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="result"&gt;Result&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together, these components form a single, managed architecture where:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runs agent runtime and orchestration.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views drive governed analytics queries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Lakebase stores durable application and dashboard state.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Async workflows and WebSockets provide scalable real-time user experience (UX).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="outcome"&gt;Outcome&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By restructuring Clara around Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and a Lakehouse-first analytics model, we established a more scalable foundation for AI-powered FinOps workflows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="development-velocity"&gt;Development velocity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;75 percent reduction in time-to-production, from 10–12 months to 4 months after replacing a self-managed Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) stack with a single managed service.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;4–6 production-ready agents now serving analytics across a shared runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="response-quality"&gt;Response quality&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;81.7 percent Correctness score (up 145 percent over the previous period), compared to approximately 65 percent in v1.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;79.4 percent Helpfulness score (up 138 percent over the previous period).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Tool failure rate reduced from 7.49 percent to 0.92 percent after adopting Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with a new tool-calling method.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="operational-efficiency"&gt;Operational efficiency&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;75 percent time reduction for manual analysis, from an estimated 2 hours to 30 minutes for Customer Success Managers and Solutions Architects.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Reduced daily manual effort for customer success, solutions architecture, and account executive teams through streaming responses and push-based workflow completion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="infrastructure-simplification"&gt;Infrastructure simplification&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Removed the LangGraph/LangChain orchestration layer entirely, replaced by AgentCore runtime and AgentCore memory, along with built-in request routing and observability. This freed engineering teams to focus on product differentiation.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We transformed Clara from a layered, API-centric AI assistant into a unified, agent-native analytics system. By combining Amazon Bedrock AgentCore with Databricks Lakehouse Metric Views and Lakebase, we created a repeatable pattern for building enterprise AI analytics experiences that stay accurate at scale without heavy operational overhead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building agent-native applications, visit &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. To see how we use these capabilities in production, visit &lt;a href="https://www.nops.io/finops-ai-agent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;nops.io&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
